We are very anxious to have our representative, Mr. Banwari Lal, visit

investigating the possibilities of supplying woodpulp, caustic soda

Memorandum of/Conversation
DATE: Apr11 2, 1948.
SUBJECT:
mahre
PARTICIPANTS: Miss Hendrickson, General Motors Overseas (Exec 1l33)
Mrs. Mellor, DS
DC/1
COPIES TO:
1/4-248
Miss Hendrickson telephoned Ds at 4:45 Friday afterneon
and said she had recelved word from thelr Egyptian effiee
that a Mr. Mahrez of the Egyotian Trading Corporation had
made an applicallon through the Egyptian Foreign Offiee
in connection with getting a military permit to go to
Japan for business reasons. She understood the Egyptian
Foreign Ofrice was sending a copy to the Egyptian Embassy,
which would take the matter up with the State Department.
She wanted to know what would be the proper course to
pursue in order to hasten the procedure as Mr. Mahraz was
on his way to the United States and wanted to leave San
Francisco at an early date. Ds informed her the proper
procedure would have been for the Egyptian Government to
874.111
present the application through the British in as much as
Great Britain is the protecting Power in Japan for Egypt.
She wanted to know if her office could do that and I maid
it wouldn't be possible for them to do anything at this
time as they did not have Mr. Mahraz's application. I
informed her the Military Permit Section, which communicates
the apnlicatlons to Tokyo, inalsts that this procedure be
C
arried out since that 1e their 1nstructions. She wanted
to know if we ever made an excention and I said in the
case of the Egyntians we had made an excention as a courtes.
2
She

=== Page 8 === (confidence 0.93)
- 2 -
0:10
She wanted to know what prompted the courtesy and I said
1t was thought, perhaps, the Igyptians were net familiar
at the time with the precedure but that they are familiar
with it now. I teld her nething eould be done until the
application was received and then it would be in the hands
of the Military Permit Section.
In reply to my question as to whether Mr. Mahrez was
conneoted with General Motors Overseas, Miss Hendrickson
said no; it was a case of trying to do a faver for him in
the hope of favors to come.

REPRESENTATION CF CHILEAN INTERESTS
Subject: Repatriation of Hiroshi (Enrique) YAMADA
Yoshiro.
The Consul General has the honor to refer so the
Department's unnumbered instruction of : arch 9. 19
concerning the repatriatin to chile of 'r. Hiroshi
(Fnricue)vA Yoshiro.
At the recuest of this office, Ir. Yamada was
interviewed b Vilitary Government officials in his
M
region and it as ascertained that he "had not pre
viously contacted the occupation authorities but had
talked to the Japanese Board of Trade who had informed
him that no Ja nesc would b, allowed to return to thefr
native country until ftr -he si ning of the peace
treaty."
Mr. Yamada has now been informed that this affiee
is prepared to obtain a permit for his departure frem
Japan and to issue him necessary documentation for his
journey upon presentation of the following items: (1)
evidence of identity, (2) any evidence which he may
have of his tirth in Chile and his Chilean nationality.
1f available, (3) photographs, and (4) evidence that
his passage from Japan to Chile via the Umited States
has been paid in full, and arrangements have been made
for all expenses while in the United States.

Reference your telegram no. 369 of April 15, 1948, and
89
previous communications regarding Egyptian applicants for
permission to enter Japan.
Upon the receipt of your telegram no. 369 of April 13,
the Department had pending only two further Egyptian applioa-
tions. One was that of Mr. David Mehrez, concerning whom the
Department sent you telegram no. 415 of April 13 stating that
/4-648
he should apply through British channels. The other wa that
of Mr. Constantine Rodocanachi, who was the subject of your oM
of April 3, 1948, and concerning whom the Department also
received a note from the Embassy of Egypt in Washington. The
Isaac Cohen application had been processed on an exceptional
basie and a favorable reply was received from Tokyo on April
14.
By April 13 the Wilitary Permit Section of the Army
Department in Washington had made perfectly clear to the
Department its extreme reluctance to utilize American communi-
cation facilities for the transmission to Japan of Egyptian
applications, or those of any other nation which has a pro-
tecting Power in Japan. Moreover, the Department had already
notified the Egyptian Embassy several times, both formally and
informally, that it was not prepared to handle any further
C
applicatione for permission for Egyptian natlonals to travel
IS
to Japan. Finally the Embassy had already been given to under-
etand that the specific applications of Mehrez and Rodocanaoh1
A

Mr. Holland called to inquire concerning the status of
the application of his brother-in-law, Mr. Michael Shhalfeleff,
for permisslon to return to his home in Japan. He was particu-
larly concerned with two problems: (1) Mr. Schalfeieff's exit
nermit from the United States, and (2) his entry permit for
CS/A
Japan.
Concerning his exit from the United States, Mr. Corkran
informed Mr. Holland that Mr. Schalfeieff's cese file had
been studied and that the Department was prepared to grant a
waiver of the exit permit requiremente in his case. Mr.
Corkran exnlained that the Immigration authorities at San
Francisco would be notified of Mr. Schalfeieff's departure,
and Mr. Schalfeieff would therefore need no ex1t permit docu-
894.111
ment.
With regara to Er. Schalfeieff'e entry into Japan, Mr.
Corkran exnreesed his belief that since Mr. Schalfeieff 1a a
permanent reeident of Janan re will need no military permit
to enter the country. Mr. Holland said that Mr. Schalfeleff
1s certainly a permanent resident of Japan; he hae his home
and
4-1548

=== Page 95 === (confidence 0.96)
De/R
一 2 -
and all of his family there; moreover, he has been offered a
Job with the Military Government. Mr. Corkran said that 1n
vlew of these facte there would seem to be almost no doubt
about Mr. Schalfeleff's being able to enter the country
without diffioulty. However, Mr. Corkran said that he would
discuss the case informally with the appropriate military
authoritles and write a letter to Mr. Holland informing him
of the results. Mr. Corkran warned Mr. Holland, however,
that no letter from the Department could be taken as a
written guarantee that Mr. Schalfeleff would be allowed to
enter Japan without a military permit.

of responsibility proper surveillance of Soviet Nationals and Soviet
Satellite kmkimnaiscikxisxmxxankixi Nationals, it is essential that advance
notification of such travel be s ent to his headquarters. All Consular

American Express Company, including his military permit. Mr.S

Mr. Jesse Briegel called Mr. Corkran this morning and
sald that he 1s most anxlous to obtain nermlsslon for six
British nationals to enter Okinawa on business. Mr. Briegel
said that he had visited the Military Permit Office and
talked with Cantain Rawls, who informed him that entrance
into Okinawa 1s at the discretion of the commanding officer
there. Captain Rawls pointed out to Mr. Briegel the fact
that Giacomo Meneace had been refused permission to enter
Okinawa on the grounds that he 1a not an American citizen.
5
Cantain Rawls informed Mr. Briegel, however, that the
-1848
Military Permit Section would be glad to cable to Okinawa
the applicatlon for the six Britieh subjects provided they
were requested to do so by the State Department.
Mr. Corkran told Mr. Briegel that the Department does
not submit applications to the military authorities on
behalf of foreign nationals unless it 1s requested to do so
by an Embassy or Legation in Washington. Mr. Corkran asked
Mr. Briegel whether he had invest1gated the posaibility of
forwarding the six applications through Britieh channels.
Mr. Briegel said that he had, and that both the British
Embasay and Captain Rawls had informed him that much quicker
results could be obtained by the use of American communica-
tion facilities. Mr. Corkran then asked Mr. Briegel if he
could obtain a letter from the Britieh Embassy requesting
the Denartment to make the anolication, and Mr. Briegel
said that he could.
Later today Mr. Briegel visited Ds 1n person and handed
to Mr. Corkran a letter from the British Embasey, signed by
Mr. F.V. Cross, Attache, reyuesting DS to present the
applications to the military authorities.

On April 16, 1948 the American Embassy at Karach1,
Pakistan sent a telegram to the Department referring to the
desire of several Pakistan1 nationals to travel to Japan. In
reply the Department informed the Embassy that since the
United Kingdom is the protecting Power for Pakistan in Japan
(see Yokohama's A-6 of January 8, 1948 to the Department),
Pakistani applications for permission to enter Japan should
be forwarded to SCAp through British channels. This was in
5
accordance with the request which has been made to the Depart-
2148
ment on a number of ocoasions by the Military Permit Section
of the Army Department, namely that in cases where an appli-
cant's government's interests 1n Japan are protected by a
third Power the applicant should be advised to submit his
application to SCAp through the diplomatic channels of the
protecting Power.
The Department has now received despaton No. 23l of
a
May 21, 1948 from the Embassy at Karach1, the pertinent
portions of which are as follows:
"The Embassy has been informed by an
officer of the Office of the High Commissioner for
the U.K. in Pakistan that it has received no
CS/A
instructions from the British Foreign Office
that it should handle suoh applications. On the
basis of the Department's telegram, the pending
requests of three applicants were referred to the
Pakistan Ministry of Foreign Affairs and Common-
DISTRIBUTION
DESIRED
wealth Relations with an explanation that arrange-
(OFFICES ONLY)
ments for handling such cases had recently been
changed so that this Embassy could no longer handle
requests for permission to enter Japan.
"The Embassy would appreciate any further
information the Department may have or clarifica-
tion that it can supply in regard to this current
change of polioy."

I have the honor to refer to the Embassy's telegram
No. 222 of April 16, 1948, and the Department's reply
No. 152 of April 21, in which latter communication this
office was informed that the military authorities at
Washington were unwilling to handle further applications
from Pakistan citizens who wish to enter Japan, and that
since the U.K. was now the protecting power for Pakistan
in Japan, application should be made through U.K. channels
by any Pakistani desirous of entering Japan.
The Embassy has been informed by an officer of the
Office of the High Commissioner for the U.K. in Pakistan
that it has received no instructions from the British
CS/A
Foreign Office that it should handle such applications.
On the basis of the Department's telegram, the pending
requests of three applicants were referred to the Pakistan
Ministry of Foreign Affairs and Commonwealth Relations
with an explanation that arrangements for handling such
cases had recently been changed so that this Embassy could
no longer handle requests for permission to enter Japan.
The Embassy would appreciate any further information
the Department may have or clarification that it can supply
in regard to this current change of policy.

SUBJECT:
Travel to Ocoupied Areas: Proposed Move-
ment of Japanese from Brazil to Japan.
Mr. Dante Miraglia, attorney for Brasiltur, and Mr.
Heotor Robino, Direotor of Brasiltur, Santa Luzia, 795,
(Telephones 22-6910 and 22-6779), called at the Consular
Seotion yesterday where they had been referred by the
Itamarati. They said that Brasiltur had chartered the
Lloyd Brasileiro SS Santarem to sail from Rio or Santos
the latter part of July via Capetown, Lourengo Marques,
Colombo and Singapore to Yokohama to carry one thousand
Japanese passengers. or these, they estimated that eighty
per cent would be round-trip passengers. The vessel would
arrive in Yokohama in September and remain there thirty
days before returning to Brazil, giving the oruise pas-
sengers time to go ashore and visit their families. In
disousging this matter with the Itamarati /Brasilian Foreign
Offioe/, they said that an officer remarked that it would
be a good opportunity to deport some Japanese undesirables.
I save them a photostatio oopy of a SCAp oiroular
governing admission of persons and vessels to Japan, copy
attached. After studying this oiroular they plan to
return and request the Embassy to forward by telegram by
ofricial ohannels their request for necessary information
to the military authorities in Japan. They hope that the
individual permit system provided in the oiroular will be
waived in favor of a group authorisation by list of names
of attending passengers. They said that their representa-
tive in Japan was the Japan Tourist Bureau in Tokyo. When
I inquired why they did not take up this matter with the
military authorities through their representative in Japan,
they replied that they did not kmow the present standing
of the Firm in Japan.

MEMORANDUM TO: Mr. Koy
FROM:
Randolph A. Kidder
SUBJECT:
Travel to Occupied Areas: Proposed Trip
to Japan Sponsored by Brasiltur.
Reference Mr. Ooheltree's memorandum of May 6, 1948.
Mr. Hector Robino, Direotor of Brasiltur, called at
the Consular Seotion this afternoon to obtain information
whioh we had received from Tokyo in reply to telegram
No. 520 sent on behalf of Brasiltur.
I opened the conversation by informing Mr. Robino
that present polioy does not permit tourists to enter
Japan. This Information did not seem to dismay him in
any way and he commented that it would then be necessary
to take only Japanese who intended to return to Japan for
permanent residence. He added that the date of the pro-
posed trip had been ohanged from July to Ootober. Ho
said that it is his understanding that Japanese returning
to Japen for residence will be admitted. I replied that
suoh is my understanding. He then asked what was the
reply from Tokyo relating to the return to Brasil of
persons now in Japan born in Brasil of Japanese parents.
I informed him that there is no general objeotion to their
leaving Japan if in possession of Brasilian passports or
proteotion passports issued by the Portuguese diplomatio
agenoy in Japan but that eaoh must have an exit permit
obtained in the mmnner outlined in soaP oiroular No. 8,
copy of whioh was given to him by Mr. Ooheltree on May 6.
Mr. Robino explained that in conversations with the
Japanese representatives who are apparently attaohed to
the Swedish Consulate in ão Paulo, he determined that
there are, among the 300,000 Japanese in Brasil, some three
or four thousand who wish to return to Japan for permanent
residence. He told me, as he apparently previously told
Guimarfes Gomes, that most of these persons are among
those Japanese who still do not believe that the Japanese
were defeated in the war and believe that conditions in
Japan are exoellent. However, it should be noted that in
oonnection with these persons who supposedly wish to return
to

=== Page 273 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
to Japan for residence, he asked what would be the posi-
tion if they decided that, once in Japan, they wished
to return to Brazil. I stated that entry into Japan
would doubtless depend on their bona fide intention to
remain in Japan and that the question of their return
to Brazil, even if they could leave Japan, would depend
on the Brazilian Government. He said he had discussed
this matter of return to Brazil with the Foreign Office
which had referred him to the Ministry of Justioe. He
alleged that the chief of the appropriate division in
the Ministry of Justice had assured him that the problem
of documentation of return to Brazil would raise no dif-
ficulties - this conflicts radically with Guimaraes Gomes'

would not be able to return to Brazil.
Rosi, rererring to the return voyage of the ship,

MEMORANDUM
On May 5, 1948, Dr. Dante Miraglia, attorney for the
tourist agency, Brasiltur, and Mr. Heotor Robino, a
director of the agenoy, called at the Consular Section
of the Embassy. They stated that Brasiltur had chartered
the Lloyd Brasileiro SS Santarem to sail from Rio de
Janeiro or Santos the latter part of July en route to
Yokohama with one thousand Japanese passengers. Of these
passengers, they estimated that eighty per cent would
make the round trip to Japan and back to Brazil. The
vessel would arrive at Yokohama in September and remain
there thirty days before returning to Brazil, thus giving
the cruise passengers time to go ashore and visit their
families.
On May 7 the Embassy, on behalf of Brasiltur, sent a
telegram to the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers
in Tokyo outlining the proposed tour and asking for infor-
mation relating to dooumentation.
In reply, the Embassy has now been informed that
present polioy does not permit tourists to enter Japan.
Japanese nationals may return to Japan only as repatriates
for permanet residence exoept in extremely oompassionate
oases each of which must be decided on its owm merit.
The Embassy was also informed that all persons, inoluding
Japanese nationals, who are permitted to enter Japan on
a temporary basis must have Individual military permits.
The Embassy of the United States of Amerioa,
Rio de Janeiro, May 21, 1948

=== Page 303 === (confidence 0.99)
FOREWORD
The Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers, General of
the Army Douglas MacArthur, is charged with overall super-
vision of the economic renabilitation of Japan in accordance
with the policies prescribed by the Far Eastern Commission.
The complexities of evolving a sound, peaceful and self-sus-
taining economy for this country in the light of ber present
position as a defeated and occupied nation are readily
apparent.
Japan's currency is decreasing in value and her stock-
piles of essential raw materials are disappearing. She is
critically short of foods, fertilizer, fats, metals, coal,
textile raw materials and vehicular transportation. Her
imports are limited to those critical essential materials
which can be procured from either United States appropriated
funds or the funds accrued from exports to date, supplemented
by the limited imports resulting from government-to-govern-
ment trading. As one positive step toward restoring Japan's
self-sufficiency, private trace has been reopened on a limit-
ed besis. You have been invited to come to Japan in the hope
that you will be able to contribute to this goal while being
remunerated for your time and expense.
Although the country cannot immediately become a corn-
ucopia supplying the world's most urgent import requirements,
the potentialities for meeting a wide variety of foreign
needs are here today. Japan has plant capacity and technical
kmow-how. Her workers, manufacturers, builders and planners
can re-establish their country as a responsible contributing
member of the family of nations if they are given proper
direction and assistance.
It is to be expected that a variety of probleme will con-
front you. Conscientious efforts have been made to minimize
the restrictions and controls necessarily attendant with limit-
ed trade and to avoid "red tape" procedures. But the occu-
pation of a country by representatives of a group of nations
is a novel undertaking in world affairs, and the reopening
of private trade under such circumstances offers little
precedent.
Your willingness to come to Japan and your interest
therein marks pou as one who is interested in progress and
who sees beyond the complexities and confusion of the im-
mediate postwar era. Your successful efforts will be a
source of benefit not only to Japan, but to all of the
Allied Mations which are today attempting to restore an era
of peace to the world.
Scarcities of food and critical raw materials in Japan
have created a highly inflated price level. Purther, the
pre-war price pattern of Japanese vis-a-vis world prices
has been completely disrupted. This distortion, together
with Japan's extremely unfavorable foreign trade balance,
precludes the establishment of a rate of exchange beteeen
yen and foreign currencies for the time being. Therefore,
trade between Japan and other countries mast be condusted
without the benefit of a rate of foreign exchange.
Weloome to Japan, or as the Japanese would say, "Wippem
we Kangeishimasul"

=== Page 304 === (confidence 0.99)
SECTION I
PERSONAL
AFEAIRS
TRAVEL
Commercial travel to Japan is available by steamship
and air from all points of the world. Prior to your de-
parture arrangements should be made for return bookings.
However, in event of emergency, you may obtain transpor-
tation from Japan to the United States on Army transport at
commercial rates on "space available" basis.
It is essential that you bring with you two passport
photographs. These photographs will be used on a personal
identification card which will be issued to assist your
conduct of business and travel within Japan.
It would be mutually beneficial and assure your smoother
reception in Japan if you would notify this headquarters,
in advance, of your estimated date of arrival and method of
travel. This information should be sent to the Adjutant
General, General Headquarters, Far East Command, APo 500,
San Prancisco, California.
ENTRY
The present commercial marine ports of entry in Japan
are Yokohama and Kobe. You will be met at the port of entry
by a representative of the Military Government Forces of
Eighth Army who will assist you in clearing customs, re-
gistering, and obtalning your billet assignment.
If traveling by air you will be similarly met at
Haneda Airfield or Iwakuni Airport.
At all ports of entry you will be cleared through
Japanese Customs in accordance with current directives of
the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers and will fill
out a "Customs Declaration Form" covering your personal
property inclusive of any shipped separately.
You may bring into Japan only property intended for
your personal use and samples of other commercial items of
è value not to exceed $500 as may be necessary and appro-
priate in the conduct of their authorizèd business.
Application to bring additional items into Japan should
be submitted after your arrival. Automobiles may be brought
into Japan only after "semi-permanent" resident's permit hes
been obtained.
While you are being cleared for customs, necessary
verification of your clearance and registration will be
accomplished, and you will be provided with en identification
card which will serve you during your stay in Japan.
Upon completion of the above you will be assigmed to
one of the Japanese operated hotel-billets which have been

=== Page 305 === (confidence 0.98)
previded in Tokyo, Wagoya, Osaka and Kyoto. Travol to the
billet aree will be arranged and necessary tickets together
th direetions will be provided. Every effort will be made
te permit you a choice of your billeting area but because of
the limited facilities currently available, assignments will
have to be made on a "first come, first served" basis.
If the vessel on which you are a passenger should be
diverted to a port other than Yokohema or Kobe, you should
proseed to the Military Government Headquarters located at
that pert and they will make necessary arrangements to insure
your travel to the proper centers for registration at Kobe
or Yokobama.
EXTENSION OF STAY
Your original clearance muthorized you to stay in Japan
for 60 days. Should you desire to stay beyond 60 days you
may epply after arrival for a "semi-permanent" resident's

reeeive necessary consideration.
You will be required to demonstrate that your continued
stay in Japan will expand the volume of Japanese foreign
trade or otherwise promote the objectives of the occupation.
Because of the shortage of available facilities in the
eommeroial hotels operated by the Japanese Government, you
will not be permitted to occupy Japanese Government operated
hotel facilities beyond 60 days unless use of such facilities
will not deny entrance to waiting applicants.
This form may be submitted any time after arrival, but
in order that it may receive necessary consideration and that
arrangements may be made to adjust the flow of new arrivals
 required it is well to submit it early. Applications will
be considered in the light of available accommodations and
the eetimated trade value which would result from approval
of the extension. No assurance can be given at this time as
to the probebility for approval of these applications.

MAIL
Use of Japanese domestic mail service is permitted.
There are no post office stations in Japan. Each post
office is a separate unit and there are approximately 75
such post offices in the city of Tokyo alone. To overcome
individual difficulties, it is suggested that inquiries be
made at one of the larger post offices where bilingual em-
ployees are stationed.
The Japanese international mail service may be used to
any country in the world, but certain restrictions as to the
contents of communications are enforced. All internationel
mail is subject to censorship. The following internation
mail services are available:
LETTERS AND POSTAL CARDS: Personal messages and business,
financial, commercial, and transactional correspondence
may be mailed. Such communications, however, must not
contain "messages that transfer currency, checks, drafts,
payment orders, or any other credit or financial instru-
ments; messages which relate to the conversion, transfer,
or disguising of any Japanese external assets by powers
of attorney, proxies, instructions, or any other means
intended to defeat regulations of the Supreme Commander
for the Allied Powers regarding foreign exchange or the
conversion of foreign assets; messages which grant or
transfer translation, reproduction, performance or other
rights concerning books, articles, plays, music, motion
pictures, or other media of information and expression;
nor messages relating in any way to patents or copyrights
except for description and explanation of the muthorized
channels and procedures for handling such matters and
except for acknowledgment of rights arranged through
the muthorized channels."
COMERCIAL PAPERS: All commercial papers except the
following may be mailed; scores or sheets of music in
manuscript; manuscripts of works, or newspapers sent
separately; all papers of legal procedure; and documents
of all kinds drawn up by ministerial officers. The
mailing of any of the four of these categories of com-
mercial papers is a violation of regulations.
CATALOGS, PHOTOGRAPHS, DRAWINGS, PLANS, MAPS AND PATTERNS
may be mailed.

You will be accorded maximum freedom of action. Cortain
restrictions that apply to Occupation Force personnel will
not be applied to you as a commercial entrant becmuse you
are accorded greater latitude of action in order for you to
accomplish your work. It is therefore earnestly requested
that you render full cooperation in your personal observance
of such regulations as are applicable to you.
Regulations pertinent to personal conduct will be pro-
vided at the port of entry. These are rules that apply to
firearms, blackmarket activities, and monetary restrictions.
Inquiries regarding these or other regulations may be directed
to the Trade Services Division, Economic and Scientific
Section, Hotel Tokyo.
The occupation has been noteworthy in the lack of un-
toward incidents between Japanese and Allied personnel.
Much effort has gone into the establishment and maintenance
of good relations, and these relations must be preserved
for the well-being of all. Experience has indicated that
anyone conducting himself in a normal manner may travel
with perfect safety anywhere in Japan. Should you need the
services of Military Police you will find them cooperative
and willing to assist you as required, and as agents of the
Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers, the Military Police
are empowered to act under the military laws applicable to
an occupied area.

against typhoid fever, smallpox, cholera, tetanus and typhus.
These immunizations should be accomplished prior to entry.
Immunizations against other diseases may be added to this
list from time to time. Should any of these be required
during your stay in Japan they will be administered at
military dispensaries at a nominal charge of $1.75 each.

A. FOREIGN TRADE. Private trade is authorized for the
purpose of making arrangements for future purchases of po-
tential exports, or for the sale of raw materials or other
commodities which Japan must import in order to increase the
volume of foreign trade.
The Foreign Trade Division of the Economic and Scientifie
Section is the agency charged with the responsibility of
supervising the foreign trade of Japan. Boeki Cho is the
Japanese Government agency charged with the conduct of
foreign trade. Branch offices are maintained in the Hotel
Tokyo, Tokyo, and the Hotel Rakyuo, Kyoto, by both the
Poreign Trade Division and Boeki Cho. Boeki Cho also has
offices in Hotel Naniwa, Osaka, and Hotel Tokiwa, Nagoya.
The manner in which limited private trade may be
accomplished is simple. Authorized exports are paid by the
purchaser in accertable foreign currencies and the monies in
these accounts are used to purchase abroad, critically needed
imports essential to Japanese economy. Trades may also be
made in barter by the provision of needed goods available
for import.
The individual Japanese suppliers are paid by Boeki Cho
at yen prices which are established in consideration of local
factors such as cost of production, ceiling prices, ete. The
Japanese Government provides Boeki Cho with yen through 
"Foreign Trade Account" established in behalf of that agency.
B. SERVICES. Providing services necessary and attondent
to international trade.
Any commercial entrant wishing to provide services such
as banking, insurance, shipping, legal, etc., should submit
application therefor to the Supreme Commander for the Allied
Powers. You are not authorized to perform such services
until appropriate license or other permission has been
1ssued.
C. RKSTITUTION. Seeking restitution of holdings in
Japan prior to the war, the restitution of which would
contribute to the economic rehabilitation of Japan.
Pre-war United Nations national property owners are
permitted to come to Japan to seek restitution of their
property, but the number of individuals in this category
is limited to those whose activities will contribute to
the economic rehabilitation of Japan.
Upon arrival in Japan the owner, or duly authorized
agent, may make application to the Supreme Commander for the
Allled Powers, through the Civil Property Custodian, through
his government's Reparations and Restitution Mission in Tokyo,
for the return of his property. Regardless of whether the
property was sold or liquidated by the Japanese Government
during the war, owners are entitled to return of their
property. However, in such cases, the owner must agree,
as a condition of the return of his property, to refund to
the Japanese Goverrment the amount of money received as pay-
ment or that was deposited in a blocked account in the
owner's name at the time of sale or liquidation. Repayment
11

=== Page 315 === (confidence 0.98)
 sueh emounts, however, will only be due after the settle
mank of any elaims for loss or damage that the owner may
make againet the Japanese Government. If the property has
besm leased to a third party by the Japanese custodian or
afmimistrator, the omer will have the opportunity of termi-
mmting the lease and obtaining vacant possession. If, how-
ever, the property has been requisitioned by the Occupation
Porses for their use, vacant possession will not be obtained
by the owmer until the property is released by the Occupation
Poreee.
In accepting the return of the property, the owner will
not be required to renounce any claim be may have against
the Japanese Government or its nationals for damages to the
property. Since the procedures for adjudicating or settling
elaime against the Japanese Government or its nationals have
not yet been determined, it is not possible to state the ex-
tent or mature of compensation which may be provided in
respest to elaims for loss or damage suffered before return
of the property, nor when such claime will be acted upon.
An owner who is unable or unwilling to assume control of
his property at the present time will not be compelled to
ascept its return, nor will any rights be may have be pre-
judieed by his not resuming control at present. Until the
property ls returned to the owner, the Japanese Government
has sole responsibility for its preservation and protection
under the direction of the Supreme Commander for the Allied
Posers, but after its return its future maintenance and any
rebabilitation costs are the responsibility of the owner.
Regulatione of the' Supreme Commander for the Allied
Powers with regard to the business activities of commercial
eatrents will also apply to property owners who wish to engage
in basiness and investment activities in Japan after resti-

PRICING
Prices will be arranged through negotiation by private
trade representatives with Japanese suppliers and Boeki Cho
and subject to approval by the Supreme Commander for the
Allied Powers. However, there is no restriction on dis-
cussions of prices between foreign and Japanese business-
men in order that they may have a mutual background of
information.
The criteria applicable to pricing are:
a. Current world market prices
b. Prices established in principal markets
c. Comparisons with prototype merchandise or sub-
stitute items
d. Negotiations in accordance with accepted trade
practices.
Due consideration will be given to comparative qualities,
styles, and other variations between present and prewar
merchandise.
The basic currency for pricing is the U. S. dollar.

BIDS
Informal bids on either listed or non-listed items are
weleomed by Boeki Cho at all times. These should be rendered
in writing in triplicate.
Suah bids will indicate buyer-interest and permit inquiry
to be made concerning the availability of Japanese merchandise,
quantity in which the buyer is interested and the price the
buyer is willing to pay.
Occasionally it will be necessary to designate certain
short supply merchandise as "Sealed Bid Merchandise." Buyers
will be invited to submit bids for the purchase of these
speeially selected commodities. Bids will be submitted in

Booki Cho or the Poreign Trade Division of General Head-

Prices and other terms and conditions of sale having
been agreed upon, an export contract will be executed subject
to final approval in Tokyo by the Supreme Commander for the
Allied Powers.
Contracts set forth sellers' warranties in regard to
commodities sold. The Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers
undertakes no warranty or guarantee of merchandise but will
lend all feasible assistance to insure that merchandise meets
specifications.

IV. STATUS CF CONVERCIAL EITRAN TS.
7. A cormerciel entrant who is authorized to enter
Japen under the provisions of this circular Is not a member
of the occupation forces or attached to or accompanying such
forces. Such entrent is subject not only to all laws and
regulationa apecifically mace applicable by their terms to
commercial entrants, but also to all laws and regulations
applicable in Japan to other nationals of his country who
are not members of, or attached to, or accompanying the

Government.
B. All commereial entrants in Japan are prohibited
from eommitting any act prejudicial to the security of the
oeempation forces or any member thereof, or any persons
attached to or accompanying the occupation forces. They are
subject to arrest by military authorities for the violation
of epplicable regulations of the Supreme Commander for the
Allied Powers, including but not limited to the following
prohibitions:
a. Entering any of the following areas and installa-
tions:
(1) Temporary enclosures and stockades for war
criminals.
(2) Dump areas containing confiscated war
materials and supplios.
(3) Military installations, except as necessary
in the transaction of authorized business.
(4) All cther areas and inatallations that have
beer iaced "off limitd" for security pur-
poses.
b. Carrying arns or other lethal weapons.
6. Pailing to comply with travel regulations pro-
malgated by the occupation forces.
9. a. A commercial entrant is subject to arrest for
offenses committed in Japan against the laws and regulations
mentioned in paragraph 7, or for violation of the provisions
of this eireular. Such arrest in the case of a United Nations
national will be made by the military police, eycept that in
arees where troops of the occupation forces are not actually
present on duty, and there is reascnable evidence that a
serious offense has been committed, or when otherwise ordered
by the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers or his author-
ised subordinates, arrest may be made by the Japanese police.
Arrest in the case of persons not United Nations nationals
may be made by either the military or the Japanese police.
b. Trial for offenses committed in Japan, where the
aceused is a Umited Mations national, will be by military
eourt. In e case where the accused is not a United Nations
mational, in the absence of specific direction by competent
authority that the trial be by military court, trial may be
sither by a Japanese criminal court or by military court.
10. All persons or organizations in Japan not attached
to er accompanying the occupation forces are subject to
Japamese sivil law and are subject to the jurisdiction of
Japamese courts in civil matters, except as modified by

VII. RESPONSIBILITIES OP COMANDING GENERAL, EIGHTH ARMY.
24. The Commanding General, Eighth Army, has the same
responsibility for the entry and exit of commercial entrants
as for other categories of individuals and will perform the
following additional functions:
a. Receive commercial entrants at ports of entry
and supervise necessary customs inspection to prevent the
importation of items prohibited by current directives.
b. Register commercial entrants 1mediately upon
entrance.
e. Check military permit, passport, and authority
to enter against list furnisbed by the Supreme Commander for
the Allied Powers.
d. Chock modical records and provide necessary
imamisation and vaccination (requirements are the same as
for occupation personnel).
. Coordinate with the Japanese Government to
arrange for the prompt movement of the individual.
f. Provide mergency attention. Except in case
of emergency or when enroute on occupation force trains,
oecupatlon force rations and billets will not be provided.
C. Provide emergency medical treatment at military
medicd facilities to the extent of avallability and limited
to that required to prevent undue suffering or to save life
or limb. Elective medical and dental care will not be pro-
vided. Medical services provided will be on a reimbursement
basis at the dollar rates prescribed In current publications.
h. Keep necessary records and notify General Head-
quarters, Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers whenever
a eommereial entrant scheduled to depart from Japan fails
to depart.

VIII. MISCELLANEOUS.
25. Allied and neutral netionals who are residents of
Japan and who are not members of, or attached to, or accom-
panying the occupation forces, will be permitted to participate
In international private tra e on the same basis as those
entering Japan. However, permission to participate in inter-
national trade does not entitle such individuals to the use
of Japanese Government facilities operated primarily for
commercial entrants, or t the privileges and use of occupe-
tion facilities herein autuorized for commercial entrants,
unless specifically granted by written authority.
26. No member of the eccupation forces and no person
attached to or accompanying the occupation forces, including
his dependents, may participate in international trade for
personal gain or advantage, or on behalf of any private person
or agency.
27. With the exceptions roted above commercial entrants
may designate Japanese, Japanese irms, or foreign mtionals
in Japan as their agents, subject to arproval of the Japanese
Government and the Supreme Commender for the Allied Powers.
28. Commercial entrants may utilize to the extent of
availabllity the International Hospitals located at Tokyo,
Yokohama, Kobe, and Osaka. Payment for services received
will be made in Japanese yen.
29. Religious services ava:lable to the occupation
forces will be available to comercial entrants.
30. Arusements:
a. Concerts and similar entertainment and sports
contests for occupation forces where no admission charges
are made will be available to comercial entrants as

forces will be available according to contract agreements
of the entertainment concerned.

MEMORANDUM FOR THE FILES
On July 12, 19!4? the Department addressed a note to the
Legation of the Union of South Africa informing it that
Mr. George Victor de Villiers and three others (all South
African nationals) had been cleared for entry into Japan by the
Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers at Tokyo.
On July 16, 1948 at 12:30 p.m. Mr. Botha, Second Secretary
of the Legation of the Union of South Africa called Mrs. Mellor
and gave her the following information:
The Legation is in receipt of a telegram from its
Government stating that r. de Villiers left South Africa
before receiving notification of his clearance and inform-
ing the Legation that Mr. De Villiers would be in Singapore
July 19. The Legation assumes that he has been instructed
to call at the American Consulate General upon arriving
in Singapore and acting upon that assumption Mr. Botha
requested the Department to send & cable to the American
Consul General there notifying him of Mr. de Villiers Theater
clearance. Nr. Botha said the Legation would be most happy
to assume the cost of the cable to Singapore.
Fu1),.894.116-948
The previous correspondence on the case is attached
herewith.

=== Page 356 === (confidence 0.97)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
XSORIEODALXXDXXDSUOXNLX
DIVI,IONF PROTFCTIVE SERVICES
MEMUIANDUM FUR THE FILES
on June 21, 1948, Miss Nilliams, DS, called the Brazilian Embassy
and talked with an officer there regaraing the Embassy's memorandum
dated Jue 11, 1948 concerning the proposed journey of Mr. Naji Tacla
and his family from Japan to Brazil. In as much as the memorandum
had stated that these persons had been granted pernission to enter
Brazil on a temporary basis "on the condition that they fulfil the
legal requirements specified by" the Brazilian Consulate General at
San Francisco, inquiry was made concerning exactly what requirements
might be specified.
The officer at the Embassy stated tnat specific information in
this regard could be obtained only through the Consulate General at
San Francisco. However, he added that it was the opinion of the Embassy
that the persons concerned were already aware of these requirements.
He suggested that, if such were not the case, they be advised to obtain
the infonmation desired from the Consulate General through personal
correspondence via regular mail channels. He thought perhaps the
requirements might include only the submission of birth certificates
and similar docwnents usually specified for issuing visas to persons
desiriny to enter other countries. He could not state exactly, he said.

Mr. Corkran called Mr. Skulstad to acknowledge his letter
一
of June 15 to the Department concerning his desire to travel
5
to Japan as an agent of his company. (Mr. Corkran had been
548
informed by the Far Eastern Branch of the Commeroe Department
that Mr. Skulatad's company has not filed an application on
his behalf for permission to enter Japan.)
Mr. Skulstad said that his company already has a repre-
sentative in Japan who 1s in communication with SCAP and 1s
attempting to arrange fron Japan for a permit for Mr. Skulstad
to enter. Mr. Skulstad added that in anticipation of his
departure he had applied to INs for a re-entry permit (he 1
C.
a Norwerian citizen but has applied for Amerioan oitizenship)
S
and was told that he would first have to obtain his exit
A
permit from the State Department.
Mr. Corkran explained that the normal method of applying
for American commercial travel to Japan 1s through the
Commerce Department. Mr. Skulstad said that they knew nothing
of that procedure and had not been 1n communication with the
Commerce Department at all. He seemed to think that his
company's agent in Janan could arrange his entry without
difficulty but nevertheless accepted Mr. Corkran's suggestion
that he get in touch with the New York Regional Office of the
Commerce Department. Mr. Corkran then explained that when the
Department tranemite to an alien in this country a permit to
enter Japan arrangements are usually made to waive the exit
permit requirementa, and therefore the filing of Forms AD-1 1s
not necesgary.

June 23, 1948.
MEMORANDUM FOR THE FILES
Mr. Corkran discussed with Captain Rawls of the Military
Permit Section the baslc problem regarding the natlonality
of the channele that should be used in prosenting applica-
tione for commercial travel to Japan when the nationality of
the firm differs from that of the employee. This problem has
been brought to the Department's attention again by Manila's
telegram 1057 of June 16, 1948.
Captain Rawls said that SCAp has agreed that in such
9
oases the government of either the firm's nationality or that
6-1648
of the employee may apply to SCAP for permisslon for the
individual to enter Japan. Consequently, American channels
may be used if elther the firm or the employee 1s American.
Captain Rawls agreed, therefore, to handle the applioa-
tion presented 1n Manila'a l057, and he suggested that the
Department inform Manila of the policy decision.
C
S

Form AD-1
Alien Departure Permit
0/21/
Application No.
UNITED STATES OF AMERICA
258
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
ALIEN'S APPLICATION FOR PERMISSION TO DEPART FROM
THE UNITED STATES
(For use by all aliens who desire to depart from the United States)
No fee prescribed
Send no money
This application must be executed in quintuplicate and sworn to before an officer
authorized to administer oaths. Each blank space must be filled in. If the space provided
is not suflicient to include the information requested, additional sheets of paper may be
utilized, with a reference to the question answered.
Five unmounted photographs of the applicant, recently made, not smaller than 2 by 2
nor larger than 3 by 3 inches, must be furnished with this application; one photograph to
be firmly affixed to each copy of the application in the blank space provided therefor. Each
photograph must be signed by the applicant in such manner as not to obscure the features.
An applicant registered or enrolled, or subject to registration or enrollment, for mili-
tary service in the United States must submit with this application a statement from the
Secretary of War or from his local draft board stating whether there are any objections to
his departure from the United States.
Other evidence may be required by the Secretary of State concerning the facts in the
applicant's case before a conclusion is reached on the application.
The receipt of the application will be acknowledged, and the applicant will be notified
later concerning the decision reached upon the application. If the permit to depart is
granted it will be forwarded to the appropriate authorities at the port through which the
applicant intends to depart from the United States, and the application number will appear
on a notification card which will be sent to the applicant. By calling upon the port author-
ities prior to or at the time of departure and presenting the notification card and identify-
ing himself as the person to whom permission to depart has been granted by the Secretary
of State, the applicant may be permitted to depart from the United States through the port
specified.
'This application should be filed at least 30 days before the applicant intends to depart.
Any permit issued upon the basis of this application is subject to revocation at any time.
Every question must be answered in some manner, as no application will be acted upon
until complete information is furnished and unless it appears that the applicant has been
granted permiesion to enter the country of his destination and to pass through all countries
en route.

Dear.r. benninghoff,
In the absence of ...r. . L. Holland, ecretary-General of the Institute of
Pacific Relations, I um riting to request permission for the Exeoutive
Vice-Chairman of the American In sti tute, r. dward C. Carter, to visit
Japan for a period of about ten days.
Both the American Institute ind the International Seoretariat desire to re-
establiah their former contacts in Japan for the purpose of promoting sound
scholarship and reseurch in the economio, cultural and sociological filds.
The Institute, as you may know, is a privately financed, non-official inter-
national agenoy, devoted to researah and publication, and composed of active
national groups in the United Kingdom, the Netherlands-Natherlands Indies,
France, China, the Philippine Republic, Australia, New Zealand, Canada,
Pakistan, India and the United States. frior to the outbreak of the war,
a Japanese group also participated in the Institute's work. It is because
we are looking toward the possibility of the renewal of such participation
that we wish to have r. Carter visit Japan before returning to the Umited
States in August.
Should permission to visit Japan be granted him, it is the purpose of Mr.
Carter's visit to confer with a group of leading Japanese soholars who are
CS/V
deeply interested in re-establishing their contacts with soholarship through-
out the world, to work out plans for the exchange of oultural and research
materials with the several mtional groups of the Institute of Pacifio
Relations, to consult and advise with these Japanese soholars on their pres-
ent researoh and publioations program (one particular mphasis of whioh is
a serious study of the development of democratio institutions in Britain,
Europe and the United States) and, upon his return to the States, to report
to the Institute on how best it can aid in promoting in Japan the growth
of sound and scholarly work in the field of intermational relations.
Mr. Carter, who holds a valid U.S. passport, is now in Rangoon, and is pro-
ceeding shortly to Lanila and Shanghai. It is the Institute's hope that
it may be possible for him to spend the period from about August 4 to about
August 1l in Japan. Paralleling this letter to you, we are suggesting to
. Carter that he apply for permission to enter Japan through the nearest
U. S. Consulate. If permission is granted by the Department, we hope that
appreprlate netificat iem may be tramsmitted to the Censalate General in
Shanghai during the period of Mr. Carter's stey there - July 2o to Angust 4.

Dear Mr. Butterworth,
On behalf of the internetional governing body of the Institute of
Pacific Relations, I ax writing to inquire whether you could give
your advice and assiatance in obtaining permission to visit Japan
for ir. Edward C. Carter, the Chairman of the Institute's Inter-
national Finance Committee and also executive officer of the
American Institute of Pacific Relations.
As you probably kmow, Mr. Carter for the past three months has been
on leave from the IPR as a Consultant to the United Nations Boonomio
Commission for Asia and the Far East. He recently attended the
conference of that organization at Ootacamund, Madras, and recently
completed his mission for the U.N. there.
)8
For the period July lst to August 15th Mr. Carter has been instruoted
by the IPR officers to visit various organizations in India, South-
east Asia, China and Japan in order to bring back a report on various
researoh projects which are being carried on in those areas as part
of the international research program of the IPR. we are partiou-
larly anxious that Mr. Carter should have an opportunity to visit
Japan on his way back from China to the United States. Aocording to
his present itinerary, he would like to visit Tokyo for about one
week some time between August 1 and August 1l.
In Japan, Mr. Carter's prinoipal assigmment will be to consult with
members of the Japan Institute of Paoific Studies, whioh is the
successor organisation to the pre-war Japan Council of the Institute
CS/V
of Pacific Relations. The new Japan Institute, which was established
in l945, has not yet been admitted as a National Counoil of the inter-
national IPR. This important question of when and under what ciroum-
stances a Japanese National Council should be admitted will be disoussed
at a meeting of the international governing body of the IPR (kmown as
the Pacific Counoil) in New York early next year. It is therefore
important for one of the senior international officers of the IPR to
make a first-hand inquiry on the spot into the organisation and program
of the group in Tokyo. This group is an independent, non-official,
privately financed society for the study of the international relations
of countries in the Pacific area and is similar in all essentials to
the National Counoils of the IPR in such countries as the United States,
China, Australia and India. It is carrying on an active program of
meetings, researoh and publication. Its organisation and activities
are, of course, kmown to the appropriate authorities in Scap. The
Japanese name of the organisation Is Nihon Tatheiyo Mondai Chosakai.
DOF Jen Un
mBE
23 208

=== Page 466 === (confidence 0.97)
-2-
During his visit to Japan, Mr. Carter would also be expeeted to
visit several other private research organisations conoerned with
economio and political problems of Japan and with the study of
international affairs in general, e.g. the Institute of Social
Sciences at the Tokyo Imperial University and the United Nations
Association of Japan.
I should mention, perhaps, that in March 1947 I myself was granted
permission to visit Tokyo on my way back from China to the United
States. I believe that my permit was obtained through the recommen-
dation of your office and that of the late Ambassador George Acheson,
then in Tokyo. The necessary endorsement on my passport was made by
the Consulate General in Shanghai on receipt of cabled instruotions
from the Department.
As Mr. Carter is now in Southeast Asia and will be reaching Shanghai
on or about July 2lst, it would be appreciated if the necessary
authorization could be transmitted to the Consulate in Shanghai in
time for him to leave for Tokyo about August lst.
I am sorry not to be able to give you more advance notice, but it
has only now been possible for Mr. Carter to work out a definite
itinerary.
Should you desire any verification of what I have stated above about
r. Carter's present position and the purpose of his travel, I believe
you can obtain it readily from Mr. James Penfield in your offioe
or from Mr. Dean Rusk in the Office of United Nations Affairs. If
you wish an outside letter of endorsement from another offioer of
the Institute, I believe you can obtain it readily from Mr. Arthur H.
Dean, a partner of the firm of Sullivan & Cromwell in New York and
Vice-Chairman of the American IPR.
It is a matter of rather special importance to the IPR that Mr. Carter,
as the only senior international officer of the organization in the Far
East at the present time, should be able to make this visit to Tokyo
before his return. I earnestly hope, therefore, that we may count on
your assistance. To avoid delay, I would suggest that you address any
correspondence to me in care of my colleague, Mr. Philip E. Lilienthal,
at the IPR office, 1 Bast 54th Street, Now York 22, N.Y. He will see
that I am kopt informed.
May I take this opportunity of renewing my cordial thanks to you for the
many courtesies which you extended to me at the Embassy in Nanking last
year.
Sincerely yours,
h2. Hill

=== Page 504 === (confidence 0.99)
FOREWORD
Welcome to Japan, or "Nippon wa kangeishimasu!" as the
Japanese say.
Scenic Japan, the land of the cherry blossoms, Mt. Fuj1
and colorful kimonos once again is playing host to tourists
from overseas with approval by the Supreme Commander for the
Allied Powers of the Japan Travel Bureau's program for con-
ducted tours beginning July 1, 1948.
Although Japan is still an occupied country with serious
shortages of materials and facilities, her tourist experts
are making every effort to enable you to see the famous
tourists' meccas with comfort and convenience.
We expect you'll enjoy your visit to Japan; but for
maximum pleasure you should keep in mind the restrictions
imposed by a war-ravaged economy. If you can accept in a
spirit of adventure the minor inconveniences which may arise
as a result of these postwar conditions, Japan will be well
worth your time.
Entry will be permitted after you have obtained a reser-
vation for a scheduled tour from the Japan Travel Bureau
direct or from a lecal travel agency or carrier. It is not
yet feasible to allow you to travel throughout Japan at will.
With the limited accommodations available at the present time,
it will be necessary for you to follow the tour itinerary
without deviation, except in emergencies such as Illness or
accident, but every effort will be made to afford you greater
freedom of movement as soon as conditions permit.
The following information is designed to facilitate
preparations for your trip and to familiarize you with the
regulations and procedures which you will follow in Japan.

=== Page 505 === (confidence 0.99)
TRAVEL
Prior to your departure arrangemonts should be made for
roturn bookings.
It is essential that you bring with you e passport
photograph to be used on a personal information card which
you must fill out on arrival.
ENTRY
You will be met at the port of entry by a representative
of the Military Government forces of Eighth Army who will
assist you in clearing customs, registering, and reporting
to the starting point of the tour.
At all ports of entry you will be cleared through
Japanese Customs in accordance with current directives of
the Suprome Commander for the Allied Powers and will fill
out a "Customs Declaration Form" covering your personal
proporty.
You may bring into Japan only property intended for
your personal use.
While you are being cleared for customs, necessary
verification of your clearance and registration will be
accomplished and your passport will be stamped "Tourist"
which will serve as your identification during your stay
in Japan.
EXIT
As in entering Japan, you will upon leaving, clear
through Japanese Customs now operating under the supervision
of the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers.
Unless specifically authorized by the Supreme Commander
for the Allied Powers, only property intended for the indi-
vidual's personal or household use and items intended and
reasonably fit for bona fide gifts whose total value does not
exceed $500.00 will be taken out of Japan.
FINANCES
All of the charges and fees for your personal accommo-
dations in Japan will be levied on a dollar basis. In Japan
you may use only two types of legal tender, a special type
of payment certificate called "Foreign Trade Payment Certi-
ficates," and yen. You should turn over to the carrier who
brought you to Japan such foreign currencies as you may have
in your possession, which you do not intend to immediately
convert to Foreign Trade Payment Certificates or yen.
You should keep dollars, dollar 1nstruments, convertible
sterling or convertible sterling instruments to purchase the
necessary special type of payment certificates and such yen
as you desire for personal outlays at designated exchange
centers. Conversions at other than authorized rates at
designated centers are not permitted.
2

=== Page 506 === (confidence 0.99)
Prior to your departure from Japan, the unused special
payment certificates may be exchanged for United States dollar
instruments in an amount not to exceed $150 or its equivalont
in fore1gn exchange. Amounts in excess of $150 or its equive-
lent in a foreien currency will be exchanged in the form of
instruments not negotiable in Japan. However, no reconversions
of yen to special payment certiricates, dollar instruments or
foreign currency instruments will be allowed.
The occupation forces use a special military payment
certificate which you are not authorized to possess or use.
MAIL
Use of Japanese domestic mail service is permitted.
There are no post office stations in Japan. Each post office
1s a separate unit and there are approximately 75 such post
offices in the city of Tokyo alone. To overcome individual
difficulties, it is suggested that inquiries be made at one
of the larger post offices where bilingual employees are
stationed.
The Japanese international mail service may be used to
any country in the world, but certain restrictions as to the
contents of communications are enforced. All international
mail is subject to censorship. The following international
mail services are available:
LETTERS AND POSTAL CARDS. Personal mossages, and
business, financial, commercial, and transactional
correspondence may be mailed. Such communications,
however, must not contain "messages that transfer
currency, checks, drafts, payment orders, or any other
credit or financial instruments; messages which relate
to the conversion, transfer, or disguising of any
Japanese external assets by powers of attorney, proxies,
instructions, or any other means intonded to defost
regulations of the Supreme Commander for the Allied
Powers regarding foreign exchange or the conversion
of foreign assets; messages which grant or transfer
translation, reproduction, performance or other rights
concerning books, articles, plays, music, motion pictures,
or other media of information and expression; nor messages
relating in any way to patents or copyrights except for
description and explanation of the authorized channels
and procedures for handling such matters and except
for acknowledgment of rights arranged through the auth-
orized channels."
COMMERCIAL PAPERS. All commercial papers oxcept the
following may be mailed: scores or sheets of music
in manuscript; manuscripts of works, or newspapers
sent separately; all papers of legal procedure; and
documents of all kinds drawn up by ministerial officers.
The mailing of any of the four of these categories of
commercial papers is a violation of regulations.
CATALOGS, PHOTOGRAPHS, DRAWINGS, PLANS, MAPS AND
PATTERNS may be mailed.
3

=== Page 507 === (confidence 0.98)
PARCEL POST. Mailing from Japan only of books, news-
papers and other periodical publications, under the
classification of "Prints," is authorized subject to
the provisions and limitations of the Universal Postal
Union Convention.
a. Weight limit will be in accordanoe with
limitations provided by Universal Postal Union Parcel
Post Agreement and/or applicable bilateral agreemonts,
but not exceeding 22 pounds a parcel.
b. Parcels containing ordinary items of merchandise
in commercial quantities from Japan must be accompanied
by a certificate or photostatic copy of a license validated
by the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers permitting
such exportation. The words "Export License Inclosed,"
must be indorsed on the wrapper as well as the words,
"This Package May be Opened for Customs Inspection."
AIR MAIL SERVICE. International air mail service for
letters 1s authorized. The rates are 25 yen per 20
grams or fraction thereof to points within 5,bo0 kilo-
meters; 45 yen per 20 grama or fraction thereof up to
10,000 kilometers distant; and 64 yen per 20 grams or
fraction thereof to all points over 10,000 kilometers.
The restrictions covering correspondence im the erdinary
international mail also apply to international air mail.
COMMUNICATIONS
Local Telephone Service
Telephone communications are available and service will
be maintained at the hotels operated by Boeki Cho where switch-
boards with bilingual operators are available.
Domestic Long Distance
Long distance calls may be accomplished in the same
manner aslocal calls.
Telephone Service to Points outside Japan
Radio-telephone service from Japan to the United States
is available from the central overseas telephone offices in
Tokyo, Yokohama, Kyoto, Nagoya, Osaka, Sendai, Sapporo,
Fukuoka and Kokura, or the Tokyo Hotel in Tokyo. Paymont
for such calls may be made in Poreign Trade Payment Certificates.
Domestic Telograph Service
A Japanese telegraph office has been established at each
of the hotels reserved for trade representatives. These
ofrices will accept and deliver all types of telegraphic
traffic which is now authorized for transmission between
points within Japan. Payment for this sorvice will be in
yon.
Code addresses for each hotel have been established and
may be obtained at the hotels.
4

=== Page 508 === (confidence 0.99)
International Telegraph Service
The telegraph offices located in official hotel-billets
will receive and deliver all types of messeges to and from
points outside Japan. Telgrams may be sent to most countries
of the world by means of radio circuits and lists of the
countries which may be reached through these facilities is
available at the hotel-billets. The use of code is authorized.
REGULATIONS
You will be accorded maximum freedom of action. Certain
restrictions that apply to occupation force personnel will
not be applied to you. It is therefore earnestly requested
that you render full cooperation in your personal observance
of such regulations as are applicable to you.
The occupation has been noteworthy in the lack of un-
toward incidents between Japanese and Allied personnel. Much
offort has gone into the establiahment and maintenance of
good relations, and these relations must be preserved for
the well-being of all. Experience has indicated that any-
one conducting himself in à normel manner may travel with
perfect safety anywhere in Japan. Should you need the services
of Military Police you will find them cooperative and willing
to assist you as required, and as agenta of the Supreme Com-
mander for the Allied Powers, the Military Police are empowered
to.act under the military laws applicable to an occupied area.
You are subject tc all laws and regulations applicable
in Japan to other nationals of your country who are not
members of, attached to, or accompanying the occupation
forces, whether such laws or regulations are promulgated by
the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers or pursuant to
authority delegated by him, or by the Japanese Government.
You are prohibited from committing any act prejudicial
to the security of the occupation forces or any member there-
of, or any persons attached to or accompanying the occupation
forces. You are subject to arrest by military authorities
for the violation of applicable regulations of the Supreme
Commander for the Allied Powers.
All persons or organizations in Japan not attached to
or accompanying the occupation forces are subject to Japanese
civil law and are subject to the jurisdiction of Japanese
courts in civil matters, except as modiried by applicable
directives of the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers.
MEDICAL CARE
All persons entering Japan are required to be immunised
These immunisations should be accomplished prior to entry.
Immunizations against other diseases may be added to this
list from time to time. Should any of these be required
during your stay in Japan they will be administered at military
dispensaries at a nominel charge of $1.75 each.
Bring evidence of immunization within
preceding 12 months.

=== Page 509 === (confidence 0.99)
We hope you do not get sick; however, if you do, medical
care and hospital facilities will be furnished you on the
same basis as to all individuals who are not authorised treat-
ment at United States Government expense. Hospitalization
charges are $9.75 per day; out-patient treatment, $1.75.
Hospitals and dispensaries are located in Tokyo, Yokohama,
Nagoya, Kyoto and Osaka as follows:
49th Goneral Hospital....

Tokyo, Yokohama, Kobe and Osaka. Catholic hospitals are
privately operated and payment for accommodations must be
made in yen. Their locations are as follows:

NEEDS
The climate of Tokyo is similar to that of Washington,
D. C. and seasonal changes of that city may be used as a guide
for the type of clothing you will require during your stay
here.
Towels, soap, toilet articles, tobacco, etc., for personal
needs are not readily available and you should bring with you
a week's supply.

DATE: July 21, 1948.

SUBJECT:
PARTICIPANTS: Mr. Anatole Muroff, Vice President of the Universal
Peltry Traders, Inc., 151 West 26th St.,
New York (Algonquin 5-2767)
Mr. Corkran, DS
DIVISION OF

 DEPARTMENT OF STATE
894.111/7-1948 H0/erk
Upon receipt of telegpam no. 209 of July 19, 1948
from Tientsin stating that Mr. T.P. Berman is a Soviet
citizen and not a Russian emigre, Mr. Corkran called
Mr. Berman's company in New York and talked with Mr. Muroff.
Mr. Muroff said that Mr. Berman has been living in
China for the past thirty years and has never returned to
the Soviet Union. Mr. Muroff 1s certain that Mr. Berman
does not have a Soviet passport, and he expressed the
opinion that Mr. Berman may be a Soviet citizen only in
the eyes of Soviet law.
Gen. Ualt
